I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Dr. [Name]. Um, well, I guess I'm here because things are getting really, really bad at work. I just graduated with my bachelor's recently, but right now I'm working at a meat processing plant, and... well, if you know anything about meat or poultry processing, you know it is incredibly strict. Which makes total sense, obviously, because you don't want people getting sick, so the USDA regulations and the health codes are just constantly breathing down our necks. \n\nBecause of all that, there's a ton of documentation and very specific procedures we have to follow every single shift. And what brings me in is that I just... I keep starting the sanitation steps, or I'll sit down to do the daily batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork, and I somehow always leave out the very last parts. \n\nLike, I'll get through the first three pages of the log, or I'll do the initial scrub and foam down the equipment, and then it's like my brain just decides the task is over. I'll just walk away without doing the final sanitizing rinse, or I'll completely forget to sign off and record the final temperatures on the paperwork. And it's not like I don't care! I know exactly how high the stakes are. I know my job absolutely depends on finishing them correctly, because if that end-of-line paperwork isn't completely finished, the whole batch gets flagged and it costs the company money. \n\nMy supervisor has had to pull me aside so many times, and I just sit there looking at the blank boxes at the bottom of the page thinking, \"Why didn't I just finish it? I was right there.\" I don't even realize I didn't finish until someone is yelling at me about it. It's just so incredibly frustrating because I know better, I really do, but I just can't seem to make myself follow through to the actual end of the instructions. It's like I hit a wall right at the finish line every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty completing tasks this way, even back in school or earlier jobs, or is this something that's been happening mainly since you started at this processing plant?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's definitely not just a new thing with this job. I mean, the stakes feel a lot higher now because of the USDA and everything, but no, this has been happening for... gosh, basically as long as I can remember. \n\nLike, my mom actually still has a bunch of my old report cards from elementary school. She keeps everything in this big plastic tub in the basement, and we were looking through it recently after I graduated. And there's this one from, I want to say fourth grade? Maybe third, but probably fourth. And the teacher—Mrs. Gable, I still remember her, she had this huge collection of ceramic frogs on her desk—anyway, she wrote this long note in the comments section. It basically said that I was a really bright kid, that I caught on to the concepts quickly, but that I needed just... constant, endless reminders to actually finish my classwork. And my homework, too. I would do the math worksheet, for example, and just leave the whole back page blank. Or I'd do the homework and then just leave it sitting in my folder instead of putting it in the turn-in tray by the door. \n\nI remember feeling so confused back then because I knew I understood the lessons. I wasn't struggling with the material at all. But I would just miss these huge chunks of instructions. Like, she would stand at the front of the room and list off three things we needed to do before recess, and I'd do the first one, maybe start the second one, and then completely blank on the rest. The only way I ever actually finished everything was if Mrs. Gable physically came over and stood near my desk, or tapped my paper, or just stayed close to me while I worked. If she walked away to help someone else, it was like my brain just went, \"Okay, we're done here,\" even if I was only halfway through a sentence. So, yeah, it's been a really long, frustrating pattern of just... not crossing the finish line, long before I ever stepped foot in a meat plant.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing things that you find interesting or enjoyable—like hobbies, video games, reading, or activities you really care about—do you have the same problem with not finishing them, or is it mainly the work and school tasks that feel this way?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, that's actually a really good question. You know, it's funny you ask that, because I've actually thought about this a lot, especially recently when I was trying to figure out why I'm struggling so much at the plant. Because you'd think, right, if my brain just doesn't finish things, it wouldn't finish *anything*. But that's the weird part. If it's something I actually want to do, or something I find really interesting, it's completely different. \n\nLike, for example, I love building these really intricate miniature models. The ones where you have to glue tiny little pieces of wood together to make, like, a miniature library or a little greenhouse. They take hours and hours, and the instructions are incredibly detailed, way more detailed than the batch logs at work, honestly. But when I sit down to do one of those, I can sit there for six hours straight. I won't even realize how much time has passed. I won't eat, I won't get up to use the bathroom, I just get completely locked into it. I'll finish the entire thing in one sitting, right down to the very last tiny paper leaf on a microscopic plant. \n\nAnd it's the same with video games. If I get a new game that I'm really into, especially those big open-world RPGs where there's a million side quests and things to collect, I will finish every single one of them. I'll stay up until 3 A.M. making sure I've explored every corner of the map and completed every objective. I don't just stop halfway through a quest and walk away like I do with the sanitation foam at work. \n\nSo, yeah, it's really confusing to me, and honestly, it makes me feel even worse about the work stuff. Because it makes it look like I'm just lazy, you know? Like, my supervisor probably thinks, \"Oh, she can focus on her hobbies just fine, so she must just not care about her job.\" But I *do* care. I care so much, and I'm trying so hard. It's just that when it comes to the paperwork, or the cleaning steps, or the school worksheets back in the day, it's like trying to push a boulder up a hill. My brain just physically resists doing it, no matter how much I tell myself I have to. But if it's a miniature model or a game, the boulder just rolls down the hill on its own. I don't know why there's such a massive difference, but it's definitely a thing. It's like I have two completely different brains depending on what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on those tasks you struggle with—like the paperwork at work or homework in school—do you find yourself feeling restless, fidgety, or like you have excess energy that's hard to control? Or do you ever feel like your mind is racing or jumping around to other thoughts?\n\nPatient: Um, no, actually. Not at all. It's kind of funny because when you ask that, I immediately picture someone who just drank, like, five energy drinks and is bouncing off the walls, or, you know, someone who is constantly tapping their pencil or shaking their leg. Like, my younger cousin does that, he just cannot sit still at the dinner table to save his life, he's always practically vibrating. But for me, it's honestly the exact opposite. I don't feel restless or like I have all this pent-up energy I need to burn off. \n\nWhen I'm sitting there with the end-of-line paperwork, like in the little office area right off the processing floor—which is freezing, by the way, because we have to keep the ambient temperature super low for the meat, so you'd think I'd be shivering or moving around just to stay warm—I'm not fidgeting at all. I'm usually just completely still. And my mind isn't really racing, either. It doesn't feel like I have a million thoughts jumping around or like I'm distracted thinking about what I'm going to have for dinner or what my friends are doing. It's more like... my brain just empties out entirely. Like someone just pulled the plug on a TV and the screen goes completely blank. \n\nI'll just be staring at the clipboard. The pen will be right there in my hand, hovering over the signature line for the final temperature check on the chillers, and I'll just freeze. I remember this one time a few weeks ago, my supervisor, Dave—he's this really intense guy, always rushing around with a walkie-talkie and stressing about the USDA inspectors—he walked past me, and I had apparently been sitting there staring at the exact same blank box for maybe ten or fifteen minutes. I wasn't looking around the room, I wasn't tapping my feet, I was just completely zoned out into absolute nothingness. He actually had to wave his hand right in front of my face to snap me out of it, and it startled me so bad I dropped my pen. \n\nSo, yeah, it's definitely not a hyper or racing feeling. If anything, I feel incredibly heavy in those moments. Like I'm trying to move my arms and my thoughts through thick molasses. The energy isn't excess, it's just... gone. Like a car that just ran out of gas right in the middle of the highway and just rolls to a stop. I just sit there in park. So no, I wouldn't say I feel restless or like my mind is jumping around at all. It just kind of shuts off.\n\nDoctor: When you describe your brain going blank and feeling like you're zoned out into nothingness while staring at the paperwork, do you feel like you're still aware of what's happening around you—like you could hear your supervisor talking or notice someone walking by—or does it feel more like you're completely disconnected from everything, like time is passing but you're not really there?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess it’s... it’s not like I’m completely deaf or blind to the world when it happens, you know? It’s more like the volume on the rest of the world just gets turned all the way down to a one, and my brain stops translating what my eyes and ears are actually taking in. \n\nLike, I’ll go into work, right? And I’ll tell myself, 'Okay, today we are focusing. We are staying on task.' I'll even arrange my workstation perfectly before the line starts moving, get my pens lined up, make sure my chainmail glove is sitting just right. But even when I start a shift trying really, really hard to stay locked in like that, it just... it inevitably slips. Because after a while, the cutting, the weighing on those big stainless steel digital scales, and checking all the different USDA labels... it all just completely blurs together. It’s so repetitive, and there's this constant loud humming from the refrigeration units, and eventually my brain just kind of taps out.\n\nSo to answer your question, it's not like I'm asleep or having an out-of-body experience where I don't know I'm in a meat plant. If someone drops a huge metal tub right next to me, I’ll probably flinch. But at the same time, I’m not really *there*. I'll just suddenly snap back to reality and realize I’ve been staring at the exact same plastic tray of chicken breasts, or the exact same line on the temperature log sheet, for who knows how long, completely without taking in what I’m supposed to do next. \n\nIt’s like the information is physically hitting my retinas, but the door to the processing part of my brain is just deadbolted shut. So if Dave walks by or is talking on his walkie-talkie a few feet away, it just sounds like the adults in a Charlie Brown cartoon—just that muffled, meaningless noise. I don't process the words. I don't process that I'm supposed to be writing down a number or moving a tray to the next belt. I'm just stuck there, staring at the label or the meat, totally blank. And it’s incredibly frustrating because, like I said, I really do go into every single shift with so much intention to stay sharp, but that blur always just creeps in anyway and washes everything out.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and school tasks, are there times during your day—like when you're socializing with friends, eating meals, or just hanging out—where you find it hard to sit still, or where you feel like you have a lot of energy that's difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. Honestly, it's pretty much the exact same story outside of work. I don't really ever get that hyper feeling. \n\nLike, when I get home from the plant... you know, standing on concrete all day in those heavy steel-toe boots, which by the way, absolutely destroy your arches, even with those expensive gel inserts I bought at the pharmacy last month... when I get home, I basically just collapse. I'll sit on my couch—it's this old, oversized green corduroy couch I got off Facebook Marketplace when I moved into my apartment—and I will literally not move for, like, four hours. I don't have any urge to get up or pace around or do anything. If anything, my roommate has to practically drag me up just to get me to make dinner. \n\nAnd even when I'm out with friends, or just hanging out on a weekend when I'm not exhausted from work, it's the same thing. Like, last Saturday, my friend Sarah had a few of us over to her apartment for a movie night. She made these amazing homemade nachos, like with the real melted cheddar and jalapeños, not the cheap canned stuff, and we were all just sitting around her living room. I claimed this one fuzzy floor cushion right by the coffee table, and I swear to you, I stayed on that exact same cushion for probably three and a half hours. I didn't shift around, I didn't tap my fingers on the table, I didn't feel like I needed to get up and stretch my legs or anything. \n\nSarah's boyfriend, on the other hand, he kept getting up to check his phone, going to the kitchen for more water, adjusting the blinds, petting the cat... he's just always moving. But me? I'm just completely planted. Even when we go out to eat, like if we go to this diner downtown that we all like, I just slide into the booth and I'm good. I don't have that... that buzzing feeling. I really don't think I've ever had that. \n\nMy mom actually used to joke when I was a kid that I was like a house cat, you know? Just finding a warm spot and staying there all day until someone moved me. So, yeah, I definitely don't have that excess energy thing happening when I'm just living my normal life, socializing or eating or whatever. It's just not how my body works, I guess. I'm always just very... stationary.\n\nDoctor: When you snap back to reality after staring at the paperwork or the chicken breasts for those long periods, do you have any memory of what happened during that time you were zoned out, or does it feel like time just disappeared and you can't account for it?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly, it’s... it’s just completely gone. I really don't have any memory of it at all. It’s exactly like you said, time just totally disappears and I can't account for it whatsoever. \n\nLike, there’s this big digital clock on the wall at the plant, right? It’s got these bright red LED numbers, and it’s mounted right above the heavy plastic strip curtains that lead out to the loading dock area. And sometimes I’ll be standing there at my station, and I’ll glance up and see that it’s, I don't know, 2:15 PM. And I’m looking down at my clipboard, or looking at a tray of... well, usually it's the boneless skinless breasts, because those come down the line the fastest and require the most quality checks for leftover cartilage and stuff. So I’m looking at the tray, trying to focus, and then my brain does that shutting off thing I was talking about. \n\nWhen I finally blink and snap out of it—usually because I realize my eyes are physically burning from staring at the exact same spot on the stainless steel table without blinking—I’ll look back up at that red clock, and suddenly it’s 2:32 PM. And I’m just standing there thinking, wait, what just happened? Where did those seventeen minutes go? \n\nI couldn't tell you a single thing that happened in that gap. I don't remember thinking any specific thoughts, I don't remember daydreaming about a movie or what I'm doing this weekend or what I want for dinner. It’s not like when you’re reading a book and you drift off imagining a scene and then realize you haven't been actually reading the words on the page. It’s just... a void. A complete blank space. \n\nAnd it’s honestly really disorienting, because in a meat plant, seventeen minutes is a massive amount of time. Hundreds of pounds of product move past you on the belts in that time. The big yellow forklifts could have driven right behind my workstation with their backup alarms blaring—which, by the way, are so incredibly loud they practically rattle your teeth even with the industrial foam earplugs they make us wear on the floor—and I wouldn't have any memory of hearing them. Dave could have walked by three times checking the temperature logs. I just have absolutely no idea. It literally feels like someone just took a pair of scissors and snipped that entire chunk of time right out of my day, and then just taped the two remaining ends back together. I just... I don't know where my mind goes, but it doesn't leave any trace behind when I finally snap back.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you snap back and realize 17 minutes have passed, do you ever feel like your body isn't quite yours, or like you're watching yourself from outside your body, or does it feel more like your mind just wasn't paying attention but your body was still physically there the whole time?\n\nPatient: Um, no, definitely not like that. I don't ever feel like I'm floating above the line or watching myself from the ceiling or anything weird like that. It's definitely just the second thing you said—my mind just completely stops paying attention, but I am very much still stuck in my physical body the entire time. \n\nAnd I know that for a fact because when I do finally snap out of it, I can feel every single uncomfortable thing about standing right there in that spot. Like, the plant is kept at a constant 38 degrees, right? Because of the regulations for the raw poultry. So even when my brain is totally checked out for those seventeen minutes and I'm just staring into the void, when I come back to reality, my toes are still absolutely freezing inside those heavy boots. And my neck will be completely stiff from having my head tilted down at the exact same angle looking at the clipboard or the stainless steel table for so long. It's not like my body went anywhere or felt like it wasn't mine. It’s very much mine, and it’s usually very cold and very sore.\n\nIt’s really just the attention part that vanishes. It's kind of like... if you leave a car running but take it out of gear, you know? The engine is still humming, the car is still physically sitting in the exact same spot in the driveway, but nobody is pushing the gas pedal and nobody is steering. That’s exactly what it feels like. My body is just parked there on the grated metal floor, holding a pen or wearing those thick rubber gloves, and my brain just... put itself in park. \n\nBut I never feel disconnected from my actual limbs or like I'm watching a movie of myself. I'm just a person who stopped processing thoughts for a while. Honestly, if I could float outside my body, it might actually be a nice break from the smell of the processing floor, which, by the way, is this intense mix of industrial bleach and raw meat that you can literally never get out of your hair no matter how much expensive shampoo you use. But yeah, no, I'm definitely fully in my body, it's just that the lights are on and nobody's home.\n\nDoctor: When you snap back to reality after those blank periods, does the world around you—the plant, the people, the equipment—feel like it's real and familiar, or does it ever feel strange, unreal, or like you're seeing it for the first time, even though you know logically that you're in a place you've been hundreds of times?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it’s... it’s all completely normal and familiar. It definitely doesn't feel strange or like I'm seeing it for the first time. When I snap out of that blank space, I know exactly where I am, immediately. \n\nLike, when I blink and come back, it's not like I'm waking up in a strange place or like the walls look weird or anything. It's just the exact same cold, bright, loud plant that it always is. I'll look around and see the same fluorescent lights—they have these heavy plastic shatter-proof covers over them, I guess in case a bulb bursts so glass doesn't get into the poultry, which obviously makes sense, but they cast this really harsh, awful glare on all the stainless steel tables. And I'll see the same conveyor belt right in front of me, which is this interlocking white plastic mesh that is notoriously hard for the sanitation crew to scrub clean at the end of a shift. I just look at it and know, instantly, \"Yep, I'm still at work.\" It doesn't look alien or strange at all. \n\nI mean, honestly, sometimes I almost *wish* it felt a little unreal, because the reality of standing there sorting chicken breasts for eight hours isn't exactly glamorous. Especially since I just finished my bachelor's degree, you know? You spend four whole years writing papers and studying and doing group projects, and then the job market is just so terrible that you end up in a freezing room wearing a hairnet, a hard hat, and a white coat that smells like bleach. It’s not exactly where I pictured myself when I was walking across the graduation stage. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, everything is completely grounded in reality. The second my brain clicks back on, I just hear the roar of the industrial chillers and the clanking of the metal trays, and I see Dave or whoever else is on shift, and I know exactly what I'm looking at. There's never any confusion about my surroundings or feeling like things aren't real. It's very, very real.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing those routine, repetitive tasks at the plant—like standing at the conveyor belt checking the chicken breasts—do you notice that your mind goes blank more easily compared to when you have to do something that requires you to actively problem-solve or make decisions, like if a supervisor asks you to troubleshoot why a scale is giving inconsistent readings?\n\nPatient: Um, oh, absolutely. It is a hundred percent worse with the routine stuff. Like, if a supervisor—usually it's Dave, he's the floor manager on my shift—if he comes over and says, \"Hey, the digital scale at station four is throwing error codes again, can you figure out why?\" then I'm actually totally fine. I can engage with that. I'll go over, check the tare weight, look at the calibration settings, maybe see if some moisture got under the weighing pan, because that happens a lot with all the condensation in the room. When I have a specific problem to solve or a puzzle to figure out, my brain actually, like, latches onto it and I can stay present. \n\nBut the repetitive stuff? The everyday line work? It is just... it's impossible. And it's not for lack of trying, I promise you. I will literally walk into the plant at 6:00 AM, having had a huge cup of coffee, and I will give myself a whole pep talk in the locker room while I'm putting my hairnet and my hard hat on. I'll tell myself, \"Okay, today we are going to stay locked in. We are going to pay attention to every single piece of poultry.\" And I really do start the shift trying so, so hard to just stay focused on the process. \n\nBut it just never lasts. Because you're doing the exact same physical motions thousands of times an hour. And after a while, all of it—the cutting the little bits of cartilage off, the weighing the portions to make sure they hit that exact 6.5-ounce target for the premium grocery store packaging, checking the expiration labels on the outgoing boxes to make sure the ink didn't smear from the humidity—it all just completely blurs together into this endless, mind-numbing loop. \n\nAnd that's when the blanking out happens. I’ll suddenly come to and realize I’ve just been standing there, staring at the exact same tray of meat or staring down at the daily sanitation log sheet on my clipboard, and I'm not taking in a single piece of information. I'll just be staring at the grid on the paper, completely frozen, without the slightest clue of what I’m actually supposed to do next. It’s like my brain just gets so overwhelmed by the repetition that it just goes on strike and refuses to process another label or another piece of chicken, no matter how hard I tried to force myself to pay attention when the shift started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. She made these amazing homemade nachos, like with the real melted cheddar and jalapeños, not the cheap canned stuff, and we were all just sitting around her living room. I claimed this one fuzzy floor cushion right by the coffee table, and I swear to you, I stayed on that exact same cushion for probably three and a half hours. I didn't shift around, I didn't tap my fingers on the table, I didn't feel like I needed to get up and stretch my legs or anything. \n\nSarah's boyfriend, on the other hand, he kept getting up to check his phone, going to the kitchen for more water, adjusting the blinds, petting the cat... he's just always moving. But me? I'm just completely planted. Even when we go out to eat, like if we go to this diner downtown that we all like, I just slide into the booth and I'm good. I don't have that... that buzzing feeling. I really don't think I've ever had that. \n\nMy mom actually used to joke when I was a kid that I was like a house cat, you know? Just finding a warm spot and staying there all day until someone moved me. So, yeah, I definitely don't have that excess energy thing happening when I'm just living my normal life, socializing or eating or whatever. It's just not how my body works, I guess. I'm always just very... stationary.\n\nDoctor: When you snap back to reality after staring at the paperwork or the chicken breasts for those long periods, do you have any memory of what happened during that time you were zoned out, or does it feel like time just disappeared and you can't account for it?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly, it’s... it’s just completely gone. I really don't have any memory of it at all. It’s exactly like you said, time just totally disappears and I can't account for it whatsoever. \n\nLike, there’s this big digital clock on the wall at the plant, right? It’s got these bright red LED numbers, and it’s mounted right above the heavy plastic strip curtains that lead out to the loading dock area. And sometimes I’ll be standing there at my station, and I’ll glance up and see that it’s, I don't know, 2:15 PM. And I’m looking down at my clipboard, or looking at a tray of... well, usually it's the boneless skinless breasts, because those come down the line the fastest and require the most quality checks for leftover cartilage and stuff. So I’m looking at the tray, trying to focus, and then my brain does that shutting off thing I was talking about. \n\nWhen I finally blink and snap out of it—usually because I realize my eyes are physically burning from staring at the exact same spot on the stainless steel table without blinking—I’ll look back up at that red clock, and suddenly it’s 2:32 PM. And I’m just standing there thinking, wait, what just happened? Where did those seventeen minutes go? \n\nI couldn't tell you a single thing that happened in that gap. I don't remember thinking any specific thoughts, I don't remember daydreaming about a movie or what I'm doing this weekend or what I want for dinner. It’s not like when you’re reading a book and you drift off imagining a scene and then realize you haven't been actually reading the words on the page. It’s just... a void. A complete blank space. \n\nAnd it’s honestly really disorienting, because in a meat plant, seventeen minutes is a massive amount of time. Hundreds of pounds of product move past you on the belts in that time. The big yellow forklifts could have driven right behind my workstation with their backup alarms blaring—which, by the way, are so incredibly loud they practically rattle your teeth even with the industrial foam earplugs they make us wear on the floor—and I wouldn't have any memory of hearing them. Dave could have walked by three times checking the temperature logs. I just have absolutely no idea. It literally feels like someone just took a pair of scissors and snipped that entire chunk of time right out of my day, and then just taped the two remaining ends back together. I just... I don't know where my mind goes, but it doesn't leave any trace behind when I finally snap back.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you snap back and realize 17 minutes have passed, do you ever feel like your body isn't quite yours, or like you're watching yourself from outside your body, or does it feel more like your mind just wasn't paying attention but your body was still physically there the whole time?\n\nPatient: Um, no, definitely not like that. I don't ever feel like I'm floating above the line or watching myself from the ceiling or anything weird like that. It's definitely just the second thing you said—my mind just completely stops paying attention, but I am very much still stuck in my physical body the entire time. \n\nAnd I know that for a fact because when I do finally snap out of it, I can feel every single uncomfortable thing about standing right there in that spot. Like, the plant is kept at a constant 38 degrees, right? Because of the regulations for the raw poultry. So even when my brain is totally checked out for those seventeen minutes and I'm just staring into the void, when I come back to reality, my toes are still absolutely freezing inside those heavy boots. And my neck will be completely stiff from having my head tilted down at the exact same angle looking at the clipboard or the stainless steel table for so long. It's not like my body went anywhere or felt like it wasn't mine. It’s very much mine, and it’s usually very cold and very sore.\n\nIt’s really just the attention part that vanishes. It's kind of like... if you leave a car running but take it out of gear, you know? The engine is still humming, the car is still physically sitting in the exact same spot in the driveway, but nobody is pushing the gas pedal and nobody is steering. That’s exactly what it feels like. My body is just parked there on the grated metal floor, holding a pen or wearing those thick rubber gloves, and my brain just... put itself in park. \n\nBut I never feel disconnected from my actual limbs or like I'm watching a movie of myself. I'm just a person who stopped processing thoughts for a while. Honestly, if I could float outside my body, it might actually be a nice break from the smell of the processing floor, which, by the way, is this intense mix of industrial bleach and raw meat that you can literally never get out of your hair no matter how much expensive shampoo you use. But yeah, no, I'm definitely fully in my body, it's just that the lights are on and nobody's home.\n\nDoctor: When you snap back to reality after those blank periods, does the world around you—the plant, the people, the equipment—feel like it's real and familiar, or does it ever feel strange, unreal, or like you're seeing it for the first time, even though you know logically that you're in a place you've been hundreds of times?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it’s... it’s all completely normal and familiar. It definitely doesn't feel strange or like I'm seeing it for the first time. When I snap out of that blank space, I know exactly where I am, immediately. \n\nLike, when I blink and come back, it's not like I'm waking up in a strange place or like the walls look weird or anything. It's just the exact same cold, bright, loud plant that it always is. I'll look around and see the same fluorescent lights—they have these heavy plastic shatter-proof covers over them, I guess in case a bulb bursts so glass doesn't get into the poultry, which obviously makes sense, but they cast this really harsh, awful glare on all the stainless steel tables. And I'll see the same conveyor belt right in front of me, which is this interlocking white plastic mesh that is notoriously hard for the sanitation crew to scrub clean at the end of a shift. I just look at it and know, instantly, \"Yep, I'm still at work.\" It doesn't look alien or strange at all. \n\nI mean, honestly, sometimes I almost *wish* it felt a little unreal, because the reality of standing there sorting chicken breasts for eight hours isn't exactly glamorous. Especially since I just finished my bachelor's degree, you know? You spend four whole years writing papers and studying and doing group projects, and then the job market is just so terrible that you end up in a freezing room wearing a hairnet, a hard hat, and a white coat that smells like bleach. It’s not exactly where I pictured myself when I was walking across the graduation stage. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, everything is completely grounded in reality. The second my brain clicks back on, I just hear the roar of the industrial chillers and the clanking of the metal trays, and I see Dave or whoever else is on shift, and I know exactly what I'm looking at. There's never any confusion about my surroundings or feeling like things aren't real. It's very, very real.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing those routine, repetitive tasks at the plant—like standing at the conveyor belt checking the chicken breasts—do you notice that your mind goes blank more easily compared to when you have to do something that requires you to actively problem-solve or make decisions, like if a supervisor asks you to troubleshoot why a scale is giving inconsistent readings?\n\nPatient: Um, oh, absolutely. It is a hundred percent worse with the routine stuff. Like, if a supervisor—usually it's Dave, he's the floor manager on my shift—if he comes over and says, \"Hey, the digital scale at station four is throwing error codes again, can you figure out why?\" then I'm actually totally fine. I can engage with that. I'll go over, check the tare weight, look at the calibration settings, maybe see if some moisture got under the weighing pan, because that happens a lot with all the condensation in the room. When I have a specific problem to solve or a puzzle to figure out, my brain actually, like, latches onto it and I can stay present. \n\nBut the repetitive stuff? The everyday line work? It is just... it's impossible. And it's not for lack of trying, I promise you. I will literally walk into the plant at 6:00 AM, having had a huge cup of coffee, and I will give myself a whole pep talk in the locker room while I'm putting my hairnet and my hard hat on. I'll tell myself, \"Okay, today we are going to stay locked in. We are going to pay attention to every single piece of poultry.\" And I really do start the shift trying so, so hard to just stay focused on the process. \n\nBut it just never lasts. Because you're doing the exact same physical motions thousands of times an hour. And after a while, all of it—the cutting the little bits of cartilage off, the weighing the portions to make sure they hit that exact 6.5-ounce target for the premium grocery store packaging, checking the expiration labels on the outgoing boxes to make sure the ink didn't smear from the humidity—it all just completely blurs together into this endless, mind-numbing loop. \n\nAnd that's when the blanking out happens. I’ll suddenly come to and realize I’ve just been standing there, staring at the exact same tray of meat or staring down at the daily sanitation log sheet on my clipboard, and I'm not taking in a single piece of information. I'll just be staring at the grid on the paper, completely frozen, without the slightest clue of what I’m actually supposed to do next. It’s like my brain just gets so overwhelmed by the repetition that it just goes on strike and refuses to process another label or another piece of chicken, no matter how hard I tried to force myself to pay attention when the shift started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, honestly, I really don't have any sense of it at all. Like, I have zero built-in alarm clock or internal timer that tells me I just lost exactly seventeen minutes. It's more like... I just snap back and I'm completely blank on how long it's been. I could have been zoned out for thirty seconds, or I could have been standing there staring at the exact same spot on the stainless steel table for half an hour. I literally have no idea until I physically look up and check. 

And in the plant, we have these big red digital clocks mounted high up on the cinderblock walls—mostly so the supervisors can track the shift changes and the mandatory fifteen-minute break times, because the union is super strict about break compliance. Which makes total sense, obviously, because standing on those grated metal floors in heavy boots is just brutal on your lower back and your knees. So, whenever I snap out of my little void, my first instinct is always to immediately dart my eyes up to the red numbers on the wall. 

That's really the only way I ever know how much time actually passed. Because in my head, it doesn't feel like time jumped forward, you know? It feels like no time passed at all, or maybe just a quick blink, but then I'll look at the clock and see it's 10:42, and the last time I consciously remember checking it, it was 10:15. But I don't feel those 27 minutes. There's no internal feeling of a time jump or a specific chunk of time missing. It's just... gone. 

And you'd think I could use the conveyor belt to guess, right? Like, oh, a few hundred chicken breasts went by, it must have been ten minutes. But because my brain wasn't processing any of it, I don't remember seeing them go by either. So yeah, I don't have any clear sense of the time itself. I'm just completely in the dark until I find a clock. It's so frustrating because it makes the shift feel totally unpredictable. Sometimes I'll snap back hoping an hour went by so I'm closer to being able to go home, and it's literally been four minutes. But yeah, no, I don't have any internal sense of the time jumping at all.

I feel like I’m becoming a safety concern instead of the reliable worker I want to be.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 4 months\n  manifestation: This has been a serious problem since I switched into full-time meat processing about two years and four months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / butcher_or_meat_poultry_or_fish_processing_worker / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S004\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: When you snap back to reality after those blank periods, does the world around you—the plant, the people, the equipment—feel like it's real and familiar, or does it ever feel strange, unreal, or like you're seeing it for the first time, even though you know logically that you're in a place you've been hundreds of times?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it’s... it’s all completely normal and familiar. It definitely doesn't feel strange or like I'm seeing it for the first time. It's just the exact same cold, bright, loud plant that it always is. And I'll see the same conveyor belt right in front of me, which is this interlocking white plastic mesh that is notoriously hard for the sanitation crew to scrub clean at the end of a shift. It’s not exactly where I pictured myself when I was walking across the graduation stage. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, everything is completely grounded in reality. The second my brain clicks back on, I just hear the roar of the industrial chillers and the clanking of the metal trays, and I see Dave or whoever else is on shift, and I know exactly what I'm looking at. There's never any confusion about my surroundings or feeling like things aren't real. It's very, very real.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing those routine, repetitive tasks at the plant—like standing at the conveyor belt checking the chicken breasts—do you notice that your mind goes blank more easily compared to when you have to do something that requires you to actively problem-solve or make decisions, like if a supervisor asks you to troubleshoot why a scale is giving inconsistent readings?\n\nPatient: Um, oh, absolutely. It is a hundred percent worse with the routine stuff. Like, if a supervisor—usually it's Dave, he's the floor manager on my shift—if he comes over and says, \"Hey, the digital scale at station four is throwing error codes again, can you figure out why?\" then I'm actually totally fine. I can engage with that. I'll go over, check the tare weight, look at the calibration settings, maybe see if some moisture got under the weighing pan, because that happens a lot with all the condensation in the room. When I have a specific problem to solve or a puzzle to figure out, my brain actually, like, latches onto it and I can stay present. \n\nBut the repetitive stuff? The everyday line work? It is just... it's impossible. And it's not for lack of trying, I promise you. I will literally walk into the plant at 6:00 AM, having had a huge cup of coffee, and I will give myself a whole pep talk in the locker room while I'm putting my hairnet and my hard hat on. I'll tell myself, \"Okay, today we are going to stay locked in. We are going to pay attention to every single piece of poultry.\" And I really do start the shift trying so, so hard to just stay focused on the process. \n\nBut it just never lasts. I literally have no idea until I physically look up and check. \n\nAnd in the plant, we have these big red digital clocks mounted high up on the cinderblock walls—mostly so the supervisors can track the shift changes and the mandatory fifteen-minute break times, because the union is super strict about break compliance. Which makes total sense, obviously, because standing on those grated metal floors in heavy boots is just brutal on your lower back and your knees. So, whenever I snap out of my little void, my first instinct is always to immediately dart my eyes up to the red numbers on the wall. \n\nThat's really the only way I ever know how much time actually passed. Because in my head, it doesn't feel like time jumped forward, you know? But I don't feel those 27 minutes. There's no internal feeling of a time jump or a specific chunk of time missing. Um, no, I... I really couldn't tell you a single thing. It is literally a complete and total void. It’s actually kind of funny you ask it exactly like that, because Dave—my floor manager, like I mentioned—he actually *has* done that. He’s walked by and tapped my shoulder and said, "Hey, did you see that batch of Grade B cuts go through?" or he'll kind of joke and say, "What are you daydreaming about over here?" And I just... I have absolutely nothing to give him. I just stand there looking at him like a deer in the headlights, because I can't even make something up. 

It’s not like I was standing there thinking about what I'm going to make for dinner, or, I don't know, replaying a movie I watched last night, or stressing about my student loans—which, trust me, I do stress about a lot, because my six-month grace period from graduation is ending in a couple of weeks and the thought of those monthly payments on this salary is a whole other nightmare. But during those actual blank spells? Nothing. Zero thoughts. I’m not daydreaming about my loans or my future or anything at all. My brain is just entirely empty. 

And as for what I saw on the belt... I mean, honestly, I couldn't tell you if a whole, live, clucking chicken went down the line. My eyes are open, obviously, and I'm standing right there, staring straight down at the mesh conveyor, but it's like... it's like the security camera is turned on but nobody actually hit the record button, you know? I don't remember seeing the meat, I don't remember seeing the little yellow quality control tags they sometimes stick on the trays when a batch gets flagged, I don't remember the water sprayers going off... it's just completely gone from my brain. 

So, yeah, if someone asks me right in that exact second I snap out of it, I just have to kind of blink and shake my head. It truly is like that entire block of time just never happened to my memory at all. There’s no leftover thought I can grab onto, no lingering image of a chicken breast... just a big, empty blank space.

I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Dr. [Name]. Um, well, I guess I'm here because things are getting really, really bad at work. I just graduated with my bachelor's recently, but right now I'm working at a meat processing plant, and... well, if you know anything about meat or poultry processing, you know it is incredibly strict. Which makes total sense, obviously, because you don't want people getting sick, so the USDA regulations and the health codes are just constantly breathing down our necks. \n\nBecause of all that, there's a ton of documentation and very specific procedures we have to follow every single shift. I keep starting the sanitation steps, or I'll sit down to do the daily batch logs, or the end-of-line paperwork, and I somehow always leave out the very last parts. \n\nLike, I'll get through the first three pages of the log, or I'll do the initial scrub and foam down the equipment, and then it's like my brain just decides the task is over. I know exactly how high the stakes are. I was right there.\" I don't even realize I didn't finish until someone is yelling at me about it. It's just so incredibly frustrating because I know better, I really do, but I just can't seem to make myself follow through to the actual end of the instructions. It's like I hit a wall right at the finish line every single day.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty completing tasks this way, even back in school or earlier jobs, or is this something that's been happening mainly since you started at this processing plant?\n\nPatient: Well, honestly, it's definitely not just a new thing with this job. I mean, the stakes feel a lot higher now because of the USDA and everything, but no, this has been happening for... gosh, basically as long as I can remember. \n\nLike, my mom actually still has a bunch of my old report cards from elementary school. She keeps everything in this big plastic tub in the basement, and we were looking through it recently after I graduated. And there's this one from, I want to say fourth grade? Maybe third, but probably fourth. And the teacher—Mrs. Gable, I still remember her, she had this huge collection of ceramic frogs on her desk—anyway, she wrote this long note in the comments section. It basically said that I was a really bright kid, that I caught on to the concepts quickly, but that I needed just... constant, endless reminders to actually finish my classwork. And my homework, too. I would do the math worksheet, for example, and just leave the whole back page blank. Or I'd do the homework and then just leave it sitting in my folder instead of putting it in the turn-in tray by the door. \n\nI remember feeling so confused back then because I knew I understood the lessons. I wasn't struggling with the material at all. But I would just miss these huge chunks of instructions. Like, she would stand at the front of the room and list off three things we needed to do before recess, and I'd do the first one, maybe start the second one, and then completely blank on the rest. The only way I ever actually finished everything was if Mrs. Gable physically came over and stood near my desk, or tapped my paper, or just stayed close to me while I worked. If she walked away to help someone else, it was like my brain just went, \"Okay, we're done here,\" even if I was only halfway through a sentence. So, yeah, it's been a really long, frustrating pattern of just... not crossing the finish line, long before I ever stepped foot in a meat plant.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing things that you find interesting or enjoyable—like hobbies, video games, reading, or activities you really care about—do you have the same problem with not finishing them, or is it mainly the work and school tasks that feel this way?\n\nPatient: Oh, um, that's actually a really good question. You know, it's funny you ask that, because I've actually thought about this a lot, especially recently when I was trying to figure out why I'm struggling so much at the plant. Because you'd think, right, if my brain just doesn't finish things, it wouldn't finish *anything*. But that's the weird part. If it's something I actually want to do, or something I find really interesting, it's completely different. \n\nLike, for example, I love building these really intricate miniature models. The ones where you have to glue tiny little pieces of wood together to make, like, a miniature library or a little greenhouse. They take hours and hours, and the instructions are incredibly detailed, way more detailed than the batch logs at work, honestly. But when I sit down to do one of those, I can sit there for six hours straight. I won't even realize how much time has passed. I won't eat, I won't get up to use the bathroom, I just get completely locked into it. I'll finish the entire thing in one sitting, right down to the very last tiny paper leaf on a microscopic plant. \n\nAnd it's the same with video games. If I get a new game that I'm really into, especially those big open-world RPGs where there's a million side quests and things to collect, I will finish every single one of them. I'll stay up until 3 A.M. making sure I've explored every corner of the map and completed every objective. I don't just stop halfway through a quest and walk away like I do with the sanitation foam at work. \n\nSo, yeah, it's really confusing to me, and honestly, it makes me feel even worse about the work stuff. Because it makes it look like I'm just lazy, you know? Like, my supervisor probably thinks, \"Oh, she can focus on her hobbies just fine, so she must just not care about her job.\" But I *do* care. I care so much, and I'm trying so hard. It's just that when it comes to the paperwork, or the cleaning steps, or the school worksheets back in the day, it's like trying to push a boulder up a hill. My brain just physically resists doing it, no matter how much I tell myself I have to. But if it's a miniature model or a game, the boulder just rolls down the hill on its own. I don't know why there's such a massive difference, but it's definitely a thing. It's like I have two completely different brains depending on what I'm doing.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on those tasks you struggle with—like the paperwork at work or homework in school—do you find yourself feeling restless, fidgety, or like you have excess energy that's hard to control? Or do you ever feel like your mind is racing or jumping around to other thoughts?\n\nPatient: Um, no, actually. Not at all. It's kind of funny because when you ask that, I immediately picture someone who just drank, like, five energy drinks and is bouncing off the walls, or, you know, someone who is constantly tapping their pencil or shaking their leg. Like, my younger cousin does that, he just cannot sit still at the dinner table to save his life, he's always practically vibrating. But for me, it's honestly the exact opposite. I don't feel restless or like I have all this pent-up energy I need to burn off. \n\nWhen I'm sitting there with the end-of-line paperwork, like in the little office area right off the processing floor—which is freezing, by the way, because we have to keep the ambient temperature super low for the meat, so you'd think I'd be shivering or moving around just to stay warm—I'm not fidgeting at all. I'm usually just completely still. And my mind isn't really racing, either. It doesn't feel like I have a million thoughts jumping around or like I'm distracted thinking about what I'm going to have for dinner or what my friends are doing. It's more like... my brain just empties out entirely. Like someone just pulled the plug on a TV and the screen goes completely blank. \n\nI'll just be staring at the clipboard. The pen will be right there in my hand, hovering over the signature line for the final temperature check on the chillers, and I'll just freeze. I remember this one time a few weeks ago, my supervisor, Dave—he's this really intense guy, always rushing around with a walkie-talkie and stressing about the USDA inspectors—he walked past me, and I had apparently been sitting there staring at the exact same blank box for maybe ten or fifteen minutes. I wasn't looking around the room, I wasn't tapping my feet, I was just completely zoned out into absolute nothingness. He actually had to wave his hand right in front of my face to snap me out of it, and it startled me so bad I dropped my pen. \n\nSo, yeah, it's definitely not a hyper or racing feeling. If anything, I feel incredibly heavy in those moments. Like I'm trying to move my arms and my thoughts through thick molasses. The energy isn't excess, it's just... gone. Like a car that just ran out of gas right in the middle of the highway and just rolls to a stop. I just sit there in park. So no, I wouldn't say I feel restless or like my mind is jumping around at all. It just kind of shuts off.\n\nDoctor: When you describe your brain going blank and feeling like you're zoned out into nothingness while staring at the paperwork, do you feel like you're still aware of what's happening around you—like you could hear your supervisor talking or notice someone walking by—or does it feel more like you're completely disconnected from everything, like time is passing but you're not really there?\n\nPatient: Um, I guess it’s... it’s not like I’m completely deaf or blind to the world when it happens, you know? It’s more like the volume on the rest of the world just gets turned all the way down to a one, and my brain stops translating what my eyes and ears are actually taking in. \n\nLike, I’ll go into work, right? It’s so repetitive, and there's this constant loud humming from the refrigeration units, and eventually my brain just kind of taps out.\n\nSo to answer your question, it's not like I'm asleep or having an out-of-body experience where I don't know I'm in a meat plant. If someone drops a huge metal tub right next to me, I’ll probably flinch. But at the same time, I’m not really *there*. I'll just suddenly snap back to reality and realize I’ve been staring at the exact same plastic tray of chicken breasts, or the exact same line on the temperature log sheet, for who knows how long, completely without taking in what I’m supposed to do next. \n\nIt’s like the information is physically hitting my retinas, but the door to the processing part of my brain is just deadbolted shut. So if Dave walks by or is talking on his walkie-talkie a few feet away, it just sounds like the adults in a Charlie Brown cartoon—just that muffled, meaningless noise. I don't process the words. I don't process that I'm supposed to be writing down a number or moving a tray to the next belt. I'm just stuck there, staring at the label or the meat, totally blank. And it’s incredibly frustrating because, like I said, I really do go into every single shift with so much intention to stay sharp, but that blur always just creeps in anyway and washes everything out.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work and school tasks, are there times during your day—like when you're socializing with friends, eating meals, or just hanging out—where you find it hard to sit still, or where you feel like you have a lot of energy that's difficult to control?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. Honestly, it's pretty much the exact same story outside of work. I don't really ever get that hyper feeling. \n\nLike, when I get home from the plant... you know, standing on concrete all day in those heavy steel-toe boots, which by the way, absolutely destroy your arches, even with those expensive gel inserts I bought at the pharmacy last month... when I get home, I basically just collapse. I'll sit on my couch—it's this old, oversized green corduroy couch I got off Facebook Marketplace when I moved into my apartment—and I will literally not move for, like, four hours. I don't have any urge to get up or pace around or do anything. If anything, my roommate has to practically drag me up just to get me to make dinner. \n\nAnd even when I'm out with friends, or just hanging out on a weekend when I'm not exhausted from work, it's the same thing. Like, last Saturday, my friend Sarah had a few of us over to her apartment for a movie night. She made these amazing homemade nachos, like with the real melted cheddar and jalapeños, not the cheap canned stuff, and we were all just sitting around her living room. I claimed this one fuzzy floor cushion right by the coffee table, and I swear to you, I stayed on that exact same cushion for probably three and a half hours. I didn't shift around, I didn't tap my fingers on the table, I didn't feel like I needed to get up and stretch my legs or anything. \n\nSarah's boyfriend, on the other hand, he kept getting up to check his phone, going to the kitchen for more water, adjusting the blinds, petting the cat... he's just always moving. But me? I'm just completely planted. Even when we go out to eat, like if we go to this diner downtown that we all like, I just slide into the booth and I'm good. I don't have that... that buzzing feeling. I really don't think I've ever had that. \n\nMy mom actually used to joke when I was a kid that I was like a house cat, you know? Just finding a warm spot and staying there all day until someone moved me. So, yeah, I definitely don't have that excess energy thing happening when I'm just living my normal life, socializing or eating or whatever. It's just not how my body works, I guess. I'm always just very... stationary.\n\nDoctor: When you snap back to reality after staring at the paperwork or the chicken breasts for those long periods, do you have any memory of what happened during that time you were zoned out, or does it feel like time just disappeared and you can't account for it?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly, it’s... it’s just completely gone. I really don't have any memory of it at all. It’s exactly like you said, time just totally disappears and I can't account for it whatsoever. \n\nLike, there’s this big digital clock on the wall at the plant, right? It’s got these bright red LED numbers, and it’s mounted right above the heavy plastic strip curtains that lead out to the loading dock area. And sometimes I’ll be standing there at my station, and I’ll glance up and see that it’s, I don't know, 2:15 PM. And I’m looking down at my clipboard, or looking at a tray of... well, usually it's the boneless skinless breasts, because those come down the line the fastest and require the most quality checks for leftover cartilage and stuff. So I’m looking at the tray, trying to focus, and then my brain does that shutting off thing I was talking about. \n\nWhen I finally blink and snap out of it—usually because I realize my eyes are physically burning from staring at the exact same spot on the stainless steel table without blinking—I’ll look back up at that red clock, and suddenly it’s 2:32 PM. And I’m just standing there thinking, wait, what just happened? Where did those seventeen minutes go? \n\nI couldn't tell you a single thing that happened in that gap. I don't remember thinking any specific thoughts, I don't remember daydreaming about a movie or what I'm doing this weekend or what I want for dinner. It’s not like when you’re reading a book and you drift off imagining a scene and then realize you haven't been actually reading the words on the page. It’s just... a void. A complete blank space. \n\nAnd it’s honestly really disorienting, because in a meat plant, seventeen minutes is a massive amount of time. Hundreds of pounds of product move past you on the belts in that time. The big yellow forklifts could have driven right behind my workstation with their backup alarms blaring—which, by the way, are so incredibly loud they practically rattle your teeth even with the industrial foam earplugs they make us wear on the floor—and I wouldn't have any memory of hearing them. Dave could have walked by three times checking the temperature logs. I just have absolutely no idea. It literally feels like someone just took a pair of scissors and snipped that entire chunk of time right out of my day, and then just taped the two remaining ends back together. I just... I don't know where my mind goes, but it doesn't leave any trace behind when I finally snap back.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you snap back and realize 17 minutes have passed, do you ever feel like your body isn't quite yours, or like you're watching yourself from outside your body, or does it feel more like your mind just wasn't paying attention but your body was still physically there the whole time?\n\nPatient: Um, no, definitely not like that. I don't ever feel like I'm floating above the line or watching myself from the ceiling or anything weird like that. It's definitely just the second thing you said—my mind just completely stops paying attention, but I am very much still stuck in my physical body the entire time. \n\nAnd I know that for a fact because when I do finally snap out of it, I can feel every single uncomfortable thing about standing right there in that spot. Like, the plant is kept at a constant 38 degrees, right? Because of the regulations for the raw poultry. So even when my brain is totally checked out for those seventeen minutes and I'm just staring into the void, when I come back to reality, my toes are still absolutely freezing inside those heavy boots. And my neck will be completely stiff from having my head tilted down at the exact same angle looking at the clipboard or the stainless steel table for so long. It's not like my body went anywhere or felt like it wasn't mine. It’s very much mine, and it’s usually very cold and very sore.\n\nIt’s really just the attention part that vanishes. It's kind of like... if you leave a car running but take it out of gear, you know? The engine is still humming, the car is still physically sitting in the exact same spot in the driveway, but nobody is pushing the gas pedal and nobody is steering. That’s exactly what it feels like. My body is just parked there on the grated metal floor, holding a pen or wearing those thick rubber gloves, and my brain just... put itself in park. \n\nBut I never feel disconnected from my actual limbs or like I'm watching a movie of myself. I'm just a person who stopped processing thoughts for a while. Honestly, if I could float outside my body, it might actually be a nice break from the smell of the processing floor, which, by the way, is this intense mix of industrial bleach and raw meat that you can literally never get out of your hair no matter how much expensive shampoo you use. But yeah, no, I'm definitely fully in my body, it's just that the lights are on and nobody's home.\n\nDoctor: When you snap back to reality after those blank periods, does the world around you—the plant, the people, the equipment—feel like it's real and familiar, or does it ever feel strange, unreal, or like you're seeing it for the first time, even though you know logically that you're in a place you've been hundreds of times?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it’s... it’s all completely normal and familiar. It definitely doesn't feel strange or like I'm seeing it for the first time. When I snap out of that blank space, I know exactly where I am, immediately. \n\nLike, when I blink and come back, it's not like I'm waking up in a strange place or like the walls look weird or anything. It's just the exact same cold, bright, loud plant that it always is. I'll look around and see the same fluorescent lights—they have these heavy plastic shatter-proof covers over them, I guess in case a bulb bursts so glass doesn't get into the poultry, which obviously makes sense, but they cast this really harsh, awful glare on all the stainless steel tables. And I'll see the same conveyor belt right in front of me, which is this interlocking white plastic mesh that is notoriously hard for the sanitation crew to scrub clean at the end of a shift. I just look at it and know, instantly, \"Yep, I'm still at work.\" It doesn't look alien or strange at all. \n\nI mean, honestly, sometimes I almost *wish* it felt a little unreal, because the reality of standing there sorting chicken breasts for eight hours isn't exactly glamorous. Especially since I just finished my bachelor's degree, you know? You spend four whole years writing papers and studying and doing group projects, and then the job market is just so terrible that you end up in a freezing room wearing a hairnet, a hard hat, and a white coat that smells like bleach. It’s not exactly where I pictured myself when I was walking across the graduation stage. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, everything is completely grounded in reality. The second my brain clicks back on, I just hear the roar of the industrial chillers and the clanking of the metal trays, and I see Dave or whoever else is on shift, and I know exactly what I'm looking at. There's never any confusion about my surroundings or feeling like things aren't real. It's very, very real.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing those routine, repetitive tasks at the plant—like standing at the conveyor belt checking the chicken breasts—do you notice that your mind goes blank more easily compared to when you have to do something that requires you to actively problem-solve or make decisions, like if a supervisor asks you to troubleshoot why a scale is giving inconsistent readings?\n\nPatient: Um, oh, absolutely. It is a hundred percent worse with the routine stuff. Like, if a supervisor—usually it's Dave, he's the floor manager on my shift—if he comes over and says, \"Hey, the digital scale at station four is throwing error codes again, can you figure out why?\" then I'm actually totally fine. I can engage with that. I'll go over, check the tare weight, look at the calibration settings, maybe see if some moisture got under the weighing pan, because that happens a lot with all the condensation in the room. When I have a specific problem to solve or a puzzle to figure out, my brain actually, like, latches onto it and I can stay present. \n\nBut the repetitive stuff? The everyday line work? It is just... it's impossible. And it's not for lack of trying, I promise you. I will literally walk into the plant at 6:00 AM, having had a huge cup of coffee, and I will give myself a whole pep talk in the locker room while I'm putting my hairnet and my hard hat on. I'll tell myself, \"Okay, today we are going to stay locked in. We are going to pay attention to every single piece of poultry.\" And I really do start the shift trying so, so hard to just stay focused on the process. \n\nBut it just never lasts. Because you're doing the exact same physical motions thousands of times an hour. And after a while, all of it—the cutting the little bits of cartilage off, the weighing the portions to make sure they hit that exact 6.5-ounce target for the premium grocery store packaging, checking the expiration labels on the outgoing boxes to make sure the ink didn't smear from the humidity—it all just completely blurs together into this endless, mind-numbing loop. \n\nAnd that's when the blanking out happens. I’ll suddenly come to and realize I’ve just been standing there, staring at the exact same tray of meat or staring down at the daily sanitation log sheet on my clipboard, and I'm not taking in a single piece of information. I'll just be staring at the grid on the paper, completely frozen, without the slightest clue of what I’m actually supposed to do next. It’s like my brain just gets so overwhelmed by the repetition that it just goes on strike and refuses to process another label or another piece of chicken, no matter how hard I tried to force myself to pay attention when the shift started.\n\nDoctor: When you snap back to reality after those blank periods at work, do you ever feel confused about how much time has passed, or do you have a clear sense that time jumped forward—like you know 17 minutes went by even though you have no memory of it?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly, I really don't have any sense of it at all. Like, I have zero built-in alarm clock or internal timer that tells me I just lost exactly seventeen minutes. It's more like... I just snap back and I'm completely blank on how long it's been. I could have been zoned out for thirty seconds, or I could have been standing there staring at the exact same spot on the stainless steel table for half an hour. I literally have no idea until I physically look up and check. \n\nAnd in the plant, we have these big red digital clocks mounted high up on the cinderblock walls—mostly so the supervisors can track the shift changes and the mandatory fifteen-minute break times, because the union is super strict about break compliance. Which makes total sense, obviously, because standing on those grated metal floors in heavy boots is just brutal on your lower back and your knees. So, whenever I snap out of my little void, my first instinct is always to immediately dart my eyes up to the red numbers on the wall. \n\nThat's really the only way I ever know how much time actually passed. Because in my head, it doesn't feel like time jumped forward, you know? It feels like no time passed at all, or maybe just a quick blink, but then I'll look at the clock and see it's 10:42, and the last time I consciously remember checking it, it was 10:15. But I don't feel those 27 minutes. There's no internal feeling of a time jump or a specific chunk of time missing. It's just... gone. \n\nAnd you'd think I could use the conveyor belt to guess, right? Like, oh, a few hundred chicken breasts went by, it must have been ten minutes. But because my brain wasn't processing any of it, I don't remember seeing them go by either. So yeah, I don't have any clear sense of the time itself. I'm just completely in the dark until I find a clock. It's so frustrating because it makes the shift feel totally unpredictable. Sometimes I'll snap back hoping an hour went by so I'm closer to being able to go home, and it's literally been four minutes. But yeah, no, I don't have any internal sense of the time jumping at all.\n\nDoctor: When you snap back to reality after those blank periods at work, if someone asked you right then, 'What were you just thinking about?' or 'What did you see on the conveyor belt while you were standing there?'—could you describe anything, or is it truly like that time period never happened to your memory at all?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I... He’s walked by and tapped my shoulder and said, \"Hey, did you see that batch of Grade B cuts go through?\" or he'll kind of joke and say, \"What are you daydreaming about over here?\" And I just... I have absolutely nothing to give him. I just stand there looking at him like a deer in the headlights, because I can't even make something up. \n\nIt’s not like I was standing there thinking about what I'm going to make for dinner, or, I don't know, replaying a movie I watched last night, or stressing about my student loans—which, trust me, I do stress about a lot, because my six-month grace period from graduation is ending in a couple of weeks and the thought of those monthly payments on this salary is a whole other nightmare. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um, oh, no, with the models it is completely different. It’s actually the exact opposite of what happens at the plant. When I’m working on those—like right now I’m building this 1:24 scale miniature greenhouse, which has all these tiny, delicate little paper leaves and microscopic terracotta pots that you have to paint by hand—my mind stays totally engaged the entire time. I am completely present. I’ll be sitting at my desk with my magnifying lamp and these super fine-tipped tweezers, carefully placing a tiny paper fern into a corner, making sure the craft glue doesn't seep out and ruin the baseboards, and I am just... I'm entirely there. There are absolutely no blank periods at all. My brain just locks onto it and doesn't let go for hours. 

And honestly, that’s exactly why the situation at the plant is so incredibly frustrating to me, because I *know* my brain is capable of focusing. Like I was saying earlier, I really do go into work with the best intentions. I will start a shift trying really, really hard to stay locked in. I put so much mental energy into just trying to be a good worker and pay attention to the line. 

But no matter how hard I try to force it, after a while, all of it—the cutting, the weighing the portions, checking the expiration labels on the boxes—it all just completely blurs together. And then, just like I mentioned before, I’ll suddenly realize I’ve been staring at the exact same tray of meat or staring down at that same daily log sheet on my clipboard without taking in a single thing about what I’m actually supposed to do next. 

It’s just wild to me how I can sit for six hours painting a tiny wooden chair without a single interruption in my thoughts, but I can't even get through an hour of processing poultry without my brain completely checking out. I just stare at that log sheet and the grid lines just stop making sense, and nothing registers. It makes me feel so guilty, you know? Because Dave will see me zoning out and probably think I'm just lazy or that I think I'm too good for the job since I have my degree, but I really, really do try to stay focused. It's just that my brain refuses to cooperate and stay present unless I'm, I don't know, gluing tiny leaves together with tweezers.
