It's kind of like... if you leave a car running but take it out of gear, you know? The engine is still humming, the car is still physically sitting in the exact same spot in the driveway, but nobody is pushing the gas pedal and nobody is steering. That’s exactly what it feels like. My body is just parked there on the grated metal floor, holding a pen or wearing those thick rubber gloves, and my brain just... put itself in park. \n\nBut I never feel disconnected from my actual limbs or like I'm watching a movie of myself. I'm just a person who stopped processing thoughts for a while. Honestly, if I could float outside my body, it might actually be a nice break from the smell of the processing floor, which, by the way, is this intense mix of industrial bleach and raw meat that you can literally never get out of your hair no matter how much expensive shampoo you use. But yeah, no, I'm definitely fully in my body, it's just that the lights are on and nobody's home.\n\nDoctor: When you snap back to reality after those blank periods, does the world around you—the plant, the people, the equipment—feel like it's real and familiar, or does it ever feel strange, unreal, or like you're seeing it for the first time, even though you know logically that you're in a place you've been hundreds of times?\n\nPatient: Um, no, it’s... it’s all completely normal and familiar. It definitely doesn't feel strange or like I'm seeing it for the first time. When I snap out of that blank space, I know exactly where I am, immediately. \n\nLike, when I blink and come back, it's not like I'm waking up in a strange place or like the walls look weird or anything. It's just the exact same cold, bright, loud plant that it always is. I'll look around and see the same fluorescent lights—they have these heavy plastic shatter-proof covers over them, I guess in case a bulb bursts so glass doesn't get into the poultry, which obviously makes sense, but they cast this really harsh, awful glare on all the stainless steel tables. And I'll see the same conveyor belt right in front of me, which is this interlocking white plastic mesh that is notoriously hard for the sanitation crew to scrub clean at the end of a shift. I just look at it and know, instantly, \"Yep, I'm still at work.\" It doesn't look alien or strange at all. \n\nI mean, honestly, sometimes I almost *wish* it felt a little unreal, because the reality of standing there sorting chicken breasts for eight hours isn't exactly glamorous. Especially since I just finished my bachelor's degree, you know? You spend four whole years writing papers and studying and doing group projects, and then the job market is just so terrible that you end up in a freezing room wearing a hairnet, a hard hat, and a white coat that smells like bleach. It’s not exactly where I pictured myself when I was walking across the graduation stage. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, everything is completely grounded in reality. The second my brain clicks back on, I just hear the roar of the industrial chillers and the clanking of the metal trays, and I see Dave or whoever else is on shift, and I know exactly what I'm looking at. There's never any confusion about my surroundings or feeling like things aren't real. It's very, very real.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing those routine, repetitive tasks at the plant—like standing at the conveyor belt checking the chicken breasts—do you notice that your mind goes blank more easily compared to when you have to do something that requires you to actively problem-solve or make decisions, like if a supervisor asks you to troubleshoot why a scale is giving inconsistent readings?\n\nPatient: Um, oh, absolutely. It is a hundred percent worse with the routine stuff. Like, if a supervisor—usually it's Dave, he's the floor manager on my shift—if he comes over and says, \"Hey, the digital scale at station four is throwing error codes again, can you figure out why?\" then I'm actually totally fine. I can engage with that. I'll go over, check the tare weight, look at the calibration settings, maybe see if some moisture got under the weighing pan, because that happens a lot with all the condensation in the room. When I have a specific problem to solve or a puzzle to figure out, my brain actually, like, latches onto it and I can stay present. \n\nBut the repetitive stuff? The everyday line work? It is just... it's impossible. And it's not for lack of trying, I promise you. I will literally walk into the plant at 6:00 AM, having had a huge cup of coffee, and I will give myself a whole pep talk in the locker room while I'm putting my hairnet and my hard hat on. I'll tell myself, \"Okay, today we are going to stay locked in. We are going to pay attention to every single piece of poultry.\" And I really do start the shift trying so, so hard to just stay focused on the process. \n\nBut it just never lasts. Because you're doing the exact same physical motions thousands of times an hour. And after a while, all of it—the cutting the little bits of cartilage off, the weighing the portions to make sure they hit that exact 6.5-ounce target for the premium grocery store packaging, checking the expiration labels on the outgoing boxes to make sure the ink didn't smear from the humidity—it all just completely blurs together into this endless, mind-numbing loop. \n\nAnd that's when the blanking out happens. I’ll suddenly come to and realize I’ve just been standing there, staring at the exact same tray of meat or staring down at the daily sanitation log sheet on my clipboard, and I'm not taking in a single piece of information. I'll just be staring at the grid on the paper, completely frozen, without the slightest clue of what I’m actually supposed to do next. It’s like my brain just gets so overwhelmed by the repetition that it just goes on strike and refuses to process another label or another piece of chicken, no matter how hard I tried to force myself to pay attention when the shift started.\n\nDoctor: When you snap back to reality after those blank periods at work, do you ever feel confused about how much time has passed, or do you have a clear sense that time jumped forward—like you know 17 minutes went by even though you have no memory of it?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly, I really don't have any sense of it at all. Like, I have zero built-in alarm clock or internal timer that tells me I just lost exactly seventeen minutes. It's more like... I just snap back and I'm completely blank on how long it's been. I could have been zoned out for thirty seconds, or I could have been standing there staring at the exact same spot on the stainless steel table for half an hour. I literally have no idea until I physically look up and check. \n\nAnd in the plant, we have these big red digital clocks mounted high up on the cinderblock walls—mostly so the supervisors can track the shift changes and the mandatory fifteen-minute break times, because the union is super strict about break compliance. Which makes total sense, obviously, because standing on those grated metal floors in heavy boots is just brutal on your lower back and your knees. So, whenever I snap out of my little void, my first instinct is always to immediately dart my eyes up to the red numbers on the wall. \n\nThat's really the only way I ever know how much time actually passed. Because in my head, it doesn't feel like time jumped forward, you know? It feels like no time passed at all, or maybe just a quick blink, but then I'll look at the clock and see it's 10:42, and the last time I consciously remember checking it, it was 10:15. But I don't feel those 27 minutes. There's no internal feeling of a time jump or a specific chunk of time missing. It's just... gone. \n\nAnd you'd think I could use the conveyor belt to guess, right? Like, oh, a few hundred chicken breasts went by, it must have been ten minutes. But because my brain wasn't processing any of it, I don't remember seeing them go by either. So yeah, I don't have any clear sense of the time itself. I'm just completely in the dark until I find a clock. It's so frustrating because it makes the shift feel totally unpredictable. Sometimes I'll snap back hoping an hour went by so I'm closer to being able to go home, and it's literally been four minutes. But yeah, no, I don't have any internal sense of the time jumping at all.\n\nDoctor: When you snap back to reality after those blank periods at work, if someone asked you right then, 'What were you just thinking about?' or 'What did you see on the conveyor belt while you were standing there?'—could you describe anything, or is it truly like that time period never happened to your memory at all?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I... I really couldn't tell you a single thing. It is literally a complete and total void. It’s actually kind of funny you ask it exactly like that, because Dave—my floor manager, like I mentioned—he actually *has* done that. He’s walked by and tapped my shoulder and said, \"Hey, did you see that batch of Grade B cuts go through?\" or he'll kind of joke and say, \"What are you daydreaming about over here?\" And I just... I have absolutely nothing to give him. I just stand there looking at him like a deer in the headlights, because I can't even make something up. \n\nIt’s not like I was standing there thinking about what I'm going to make for dinner, or, I don't know, replaying a movie I watched last night, or stressing about my student loans—which, trust me, I do stress about a lot, because my six-month grace period from graduation is ending in a couple of weeks and the thought of those monthly payments on this salary is a whole other nightmare. But during those actual blank spells? Nothing. Zero thoughts. I’m not daydreaming about my loans or my future or anything at all. My brain is just entirely empty. \n\nAnd as for what I saw on the belt... I mean, honestly, I couldn't tell you if a whole, live, clucking chicken went down the line. My eyes are open, obviously, and I'm standing right there, staring straight down at the mesh conveyor, but it's like... it's like the security camera is turned on but nobody actually hit the record button, you know? I don't remember seeing the meat, I don't remember seeing the little yellow quality control tags they sometimes stick on the trays when a batch gets flagged, I don't remember the water sprayers going off... it's just completely gone from my brain. \n\nSo, yeah, if someone asks me right in that exact second I snap out of it, I just have to kind of blink and shake my head. It truly is like that entire block of time just never happened to my memory at all. There’s no leftover thought I can grab onto, no lingering image of a chicken breast... just a big, empty blank space.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned earlier that you can sit for six hours straight working on those miniature models without realizing time has passed. During those long sessions on the models, do you ever experience those same blank periods where your mind goes completely empty and you lose chunks of time, or does your mind stay engaged and present the whole time you're working on them?\n\nPatient: Um, oh, no, with the models it is completely different. It’s actually the exact opposite of what happens at the plant. When I’m working on those—like right now I’m building this 1:24 scale miniature greenhouse, which has all these tiny, delicate little paper leaves and microscopic terracotta pots that you have to paint by hand—my mind stays totally engaged the entire time. I am completely present. I’ll be sitting at my desk with my magnifying lamp and these super fine-tipped tweezers, carefully placing a tiny paper fern into a corner, making sure the craft glue doesn't seep out and ruin the baseboards, and I am just... I'm entirely there. There are absolutely no blank periods at all. My brain just locks onto it and doesn't let go for hours. \n\nAnd honestly, that’s exactly why the situation at the plant is so incredibly frustrating to me, because I *know* my brain is capable of focusing. Like I was saying earlier, I really do go into work with the best intentions. I will start a shift trying really, really hard to stay locked in. I put so much mental energy into just trying to be a good worker and pay attention to the line. \n\nBut no matter how hard I try to force it, after a while, all of it—the cutting, the weighing the portions, checking the expiration labels on the boxes—it all just completely blurs together. And then, just like I mentioned before, I’ll suddenly realize I’ve been staring at the exact same tray of meat or staring down at that same daily log sheet on my clipboard without taking in a single thing about what I’m actually supposed to do next. \n\nIt’s just wild to me how I can sit for six hours painting a tiny wooden chair without a single interruption in my thoughts, but I can't even get through an hour of processing poultry without my brain completely checking out. I just stare at that log sheet and the grid lines just stop making sense, and nothing registers. It makes me feel so guilty, you know? Because Dave will see me zoning out and probably think I'm just lazy or that I think I'm too good for the job since I have my degree, but I really, really do try to stay focused. It's just that my brain refuses to cooperate and stay present unless I'm, I don't know, gluing tiny leaves together with tweezers.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no, I could tell them exactly what I was doing down to the absolute tiniest detail. It’s not a blank at all. Because when I'm doing that, my brain is actually, like, actively working and recording everything, you know? I'm constantly thinking ahead to the next step.

It's just... gone. \n\nAnd you'd think I could use the conveyor belt to guess, right? Like, oh, a few hundred chicken breasts went by, it must have been ten minutes. Patient demonstrates clear memory encoding and retrieval during engaging tasks (miniature models - can recall details), but selective attention/encoding failure during routine tasks. This is consistent with a specific attention/encoding deficit during non-preferred tasks, not dissociative amnesia. It definitely doesn't feel strange or like I'm seeing it for the first time. When I snap out of that blank space, I know exactly where I am, immediately. \n\nLike, when I blink and come back, it's not like I'm waking up in a strange place or like the walls look weird or anything. It's just the exact same cold, bright, loud plant that it always is. I'll look around and see the same fluorescent lights—they have these heavy plastic shatter-proof covers over them, I guess in case a bulb bursts so glass doesn't get into the poultry, which obviously makes sense, but they cast this really harsh, awful glare on all the stainless steel tables. And I'll see the same conveyor belt right in front of me, which is this interlocking white plastic mesh that is notoriously hard for the sanitation crew to scrub clean at the end of a shift. I just look at it and know, instantly, \"Yep, I'm still at work.\" It doesn't look alien or strange at all. \n\nI mean, honestly, sometimes I almost *wish* it felt a little unreal, because the reality of standing there sorting chicken breasts for eight hours isn't exactly glamorous. Especially since I just finished my bachelor's degree, you know? You spend four whole years writing papers and studying and doing group projects, and then the job market is just so terrible that you end up in a freezing room wearing a hairnet, a hard hat, and a white coat that smells like bleach. It’s not exactly where I pictured myself when I was walking across the graduation stage. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, everything is completely grounded in reality. The second my brain clicks back on, I just hear the roar of the industrial chillers and the clanking of the metal trays, and I see Dave or whoever else is on shift, and I know exactly what I'm looking at. There's never any confusion about my surroundings or feeling like things aren't real. It's very, very real.\n\nDoctor: When you're doing those routine, repetitive tasks at the plant—like standing at the conveyor belt checking the chicken breasts—do you notice that your mind goes blank more easily compared to when you have to do something that requires you to actively problem-solve or make decisions, like if a supervisor asks you to troubleshoot why a scale is giving inconsistent readings?\n\nPatient: Um, oh, absolutely. It is a hundred percent worse with the routine stuff. Like, if a supervisor—usually it's Dave, he's the floor manager on my shift—if he comes over and says, \"Hey, the digital scale at station four is throwing error codes again, can you figure out why?\" then I'm actually totally fine. I can engage with that. I'll go over, check the tare weight, look at the calibration settings, maybe see if some moisture got under the weighing pan, because that happens a lot with all the condensation in the room. When I have a specific problem to solve or a puzzle to figure out, my brain actually, like, latches onto it and I can stay present. \n\nBut the repetitive stuff? The everyday line work? It is just... it's impossible. And it's not for lack of trying, I promise you. I will literally walk into the plant at 6:00 AM, having had a huge cup of coffee, and I will give myself a whole pep talk in the locker room while I'm putting my hairnet and my hard hat on. I'll tell myself, \"Okay, today we are going to stay locked in. We are going to pay attention to every single piece of poultry.\" And I really do start the shift trying so, so hard to just stay focused on the process. \n\nBut it just never lasts. Because you're doing the exact same physical motions thousands of times an hour. And after a while, all of it—the cutting the little bits of cartilage off, the weighing the portions to make sure they hit that exact 6.5-ounce target for the premium grocery store packaging, checking the expiration labels on the outgoing boxes to make sure the ink didn't smear from the humidity—it all just completely blurs together into this endless, mind-numbing loop. \n\nAnd that's when the blanking out happens. I’ll suddenly come to and realize I’ve just been standing there, staring at the exact same tray of meat or staring down at the daily sanitation log sheet on my clipboard, and I'm not taking in a single piece of information. I'll just be staring at the grid on the paper, completely frozen, without the slightest clue of what I’m actually supposed to do next. It’s like my brain just gets so overwhelmed by the repetition that it just goes on strike and refuses to process another label or another piece of chicken, no matter how hard I tried to force myself to pay attention when the shift started.\n\nDoctor: When you snap back to reality after those blank periods at work, do you ever feel confused about how much time has passed, or do you have a clear sense that time jumped forward—like you know 17 minutes went by even though you have no memory of it?\n\nPatient: Um, honestly, I really don't have any sense of it at all. Like, I have zero built-in alarm clock or internal timer that tells me I just lost exactly seventeen minutes. It's more like... I just snap back and I'm completely blank on how long it's been. I could have been zoned out for thirty seconds, or I could have been standing there staring at the exact same spot on the stainless steel table for half an hour. I literally have no idea until I physically look up and check. \n\nAnd in the plant, we have these big red digital clocks mounted high up on the cinderblock walls—mostly so the supervisors can track the shift changes and the mandatory fifteen-minute break times, because the union is super strict about break compliance. Which makes total sense, obviously, because standing on those grated metal floors in heavy boots is just brutal on your lower back and your knees. So, whenever I snap out of my little void, my first instinct is always to immediately dart my eyes up to the red numbers on the wall. \n\nThat's really the only way I ever know how much time actually passed. Because in my head, it doesn't feel like time jumped forward, you know? It feels like no time passed at all, or maybe just a quick blink, but then I'll look at the clock and see it's 10:42, and the last time I consciously remember checking it, it was 10:15. But I don't feel those 27 minutes. There's no internal feeling of a time jump or a specific chunk of time missing. It's just... gone. \n\nAnd you'd think I could use the conveyor belt to guess, right? Like, oh, a few hundred chicken breasts went by, it must have been ten minutes. But because my brain wasn't processing any of it, I don't remember seeing them go by either. So yeah, I don't have any clear sense of the time itself. I'm just completely in the dark until I find a clock. It's so frustrating because it makes the shift feel totally unpredictable. Sometimes I'll snap back hoping an hour went by so I'm closer to being able to go home, and it's literally been four minutes. But yeah, no, I don't have any internal sense of the time jumping at all.\n\nDoctor: When you snap back to reality after those blank periods at work, if someone asked you right then, 'What were you just thinking about?' or 'What did you see on the conveyor belt while you were standing there?'—could you describe anything, or is it truly like that time period never happened to your memory at all?\n\nPatient: Um, no, I... I really couldn't tell you a single thing. It is literally a complete and total void. It’s actually kind of funny you ask it exactly like that, because Dave—my floor manager, like I mentioned—he actually *has* done that. He’s walked by and tapped my shoulder and said, \"Hey, did you see that batch of Grade B cuts go through?\" or he'll kind of joke and say, \"What are you daydreaming about over here?\" And I just... I have absolutely nothing to give him. I just stand there looking at him like a deer in the headlights, because I can't even make something up. \n\nIt’s not like I was standing there thinking about what I'm going to make for dinner, or, I don't know, replaying a movie I watched last night, or stressing about my student loans—which, trust me, I do stress about a lot, because my six-month grace period from graduation is ending in a couple of weeks and the thought of those monthly payments on this salary is a whole other nightmare. But during those actual blank spells? Nothing. Zero thoughts. I’m not daydreaming about my loans or my future or anything at all. My brain is just entirely empty. \n\nAnd as for what I saw on the belt... I mean, honestly, I couldn't tell you if a whole, live, clucking chicken went down the line. My eyes are open, obviously, and I'm standing right there, staring straight down at the mesh conveyor, but it's like... it's like the security camera is turned on but nobody actually hit the record button, you know? I don't remember seeing the meat, I don't remember seeing the little yellow quality control tags they sometimes stick on the trays when a batch gets flagged, I don't remember the water sprayers going off... it's just completely gone from my brain. \n\nSo, yeah, if someone asks me right in that exact second I snap out of it, I just have to kind of blink and shake my head. It truly is like that entire block of time just never happened to my memory at all. There’s no leftover thought I can grab onto, no lingering image of a chicken breast... just a big, empty blank space.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned earlier that you can sit for six hours straight working on those miniature models without realizing time has passed. During those long sessions on the models, do you ever experience those same blank periods where your mind goes completely empty and you lose chunks of time, or does your mind stay engaged and present the whole time you're working on them?\n\nPatient: Um, oh, no, with the models it is completely different. It’s actually the exact opposite of what happens at the plant. When I’m working on those—like right now I’m building this 1:24 scale miniature greenhouse, which has all these tiny, delicate little paper leaves and microscopic terracotta pots that you have to paint by hand—my mind stays totally engaged the entire time. I am completely present. I’ll be sitting at my desk with my magnifying lamp and these super fine-tipped tweezers, carefully placing a tiny paper fern into a corner, making sure the craft glue doesn't seep out and ruin the baseboards, and I am just... I'm entirely there. There are absolutely no blank periods at all. My brain just locks onto it and doesn't let go for hours. \n\nAnd honestly, that’s exactly why the situation at the plant is so incredibly frustrating to me, because I *know* my brain is capable of focusing. Like I was saying earlier, I really do go into work with the best intentions. I will start a shift trying really, really hard to stay locked in. I put so much mental energy into just trying to be a good worker and pay attention to the line. \n\nBut no matter how hard I try to force it, after a while, all of it—the cutting, the weighing the portions, checking the expiration labels on the boxes—it all just completely blurs together. And then, just like I mentioned before, I’ll suddenly realize I’ve been staring at the exact same tray of meat or staring down at that same daily log sheet on my clipboard without taking in a single thing about what I’m actually supposed to do next. \n\nIt’s just wild to me how I can sit for six hours painting a tiny wooden chair without a single interruption in my thoughts, but I can't even get through an hour of processing poultry without my brain completely checking out. I just stare at that log sheet and the grid lines just stop making sense, and nothing registers. It makes me feel so guilty, you know? Because Dave will see me zoning out and probably think I'm just lazy or that I think I'm too good for the job since I have my degree, but I really, really do try to stay focused. It's just that my brain refuses to cooperate and stay present unless I'm, I don't know, gluing tiny leaves together with tweezers.\n\nDoctor: When you're working on those miniature models for six hours straight and you're completely locked in—if someone interrupted you and asked you to describe what you were just thinking about or what you just glued together in the last five minutes, could you tell them, or would that also be a complete blank like it is at the plant?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I could tell them exactly what I was doing down to the absolute tiniest detail. It’s not a blank at all. Because when I'm doing that, my brain is actually, like, actively working and recording everything, you know? I'm constantly thinking ahead to the next step. \n\nLike, just the other day, my roommate Sarah came into my room right in the middle of a session. She just burst in to ask me if I had paid the electric bill yet—which, honestly, kind of annoyed me because we have a shared spreadsheet for utilities and if she just checked it she'd see I paid it on Tuesday, but that's a whole different thing. But anyway, when she interrupted me, I didn't have that deer-in-the-headlights feeling at all. I knew exactly what I was doing. I could have told her, \"I was just mixing a tiny drop of burnt umber acrylic paint with a little water to make a wash for these terracotta pots so they look weathered, and I was just debating whether I should use the tweezers or a toothpick to place the fake moss around the base.\" \n\nI know every single thought I just had, and I know exactly what I physically just did in the last five minutes, the last ten minutes, the last hour. I'm completely aware of every second, every little paper leaf I glued, every thought I had about the placement. \n\nSo it’s completely different from the plant. At the plant, like I was saying, it’s just a total void where the time and the chicken and everything just gets deleted from my brain. But with the miniatures, the tape is running the whole time. It’s so clear. I mean, I really wish I could just bottle that feeling, that level of clarity and focus, and take it with me to my shifts on the processing floor, because it would make my life so much easier. But it just doesn't work that way. When I'm at my desk with my crafts, I'm fully there."
